Yeah… yeah, I think so. It’s not like I’m crying all day or anything, but most days I wake up and there’s this really heavy empty feeling, like there’s nothing in me. And once my energy dropped the way it did, it started feeling hopeless, like I’d already messed everything up.

I keep thinking I ruined my future in healthcare before it even really started. So then I start thinking, if I can’t even function now, how am I supposed to build a real career or become someone people can rely on?

It’s there most of the day, almost every day lately. Even when I’m around people or doing what I’m supposed to do, underneath it I just feel kind of empty and like there isn’t much point because I already fell behind or damaged my chances somehow.

I don’t know if I can say that for sure. Like, I’m trying to think of examples, but I feel kind of blank when you ask it that way.

I haven’t really been paying attention to whether things feel “rewarding” or not. It’s more like I’ll think about doing something with friends or something I normally would do, and then I’m already tired just thinking about getting myself there, or making conversation, or acting normal. So I can’t tell if the thing itself doesn’t feel good anymore, or if I’m just too worn down to even get to the point where I’d know.

I guess I’m not looking forward to stuff much, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not enjoying it. Sometimes I feel like if someone put me in the situation, maybe I’d enjoy parts of it, but I’m not really seeking it out. So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes and make it sound clearer than it is.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… the moving slowly part, definitely. I didn’t really notice it at first as a “symptom,” I just thought I was tired or distracted, but people at work started saying things like I looked slowed down, or asking if I was okay because I wasn’t responding like I normally do.

And I could feel it too, once I paid attention. Even really basic things were taking me way longer than they should. Like putting a blood pressure cuff away after using it, something I’ve done a million times, I’d just be standing there doing it so slowly, like my hands weren’t catching up with what I was trying to do. Or if someone asked me a simple question, I’d need this weird extra amount of time to process it and answer, even if I knew the answer.

It’s embarrassing, honestly, because in that kind of work you’re supposed to be quick and alert, and I felt like everyone could see I was lagging behind. I don’t think it was so much that I couldn’t sit still. It was more like everything in me got slowed down.

Like, I’m only 18, I know that, but it feels like I should be able to handle things better, and instead I’m barely getting through basic stuff. So then I start thinking, if I can’t even function now, how am I supposed to build a real career or become someone people can rely on?\n\nIt’s there most of the day, almost every day lately. Even when I’m around people or doing what I’m supposed to do, underneath it I just feel kind of empty and like there isn’t much point because I already fell behind or damaged my chances somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or time with friends, don’t feel as rewarding or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. Like, I’m trying to think of examples, but I feel kind of blank when you ask it that way.\n\nI haven’t really been paying attention to whether things feel “rewarding” or not. It’s more like I’ll think about doing something with friends or something I normally would do, and then I’m already tired just thinking about getting myself there, or making conversation, or acting normal. So I can’t tell if the thing itself doesn’t feel good anymore, or if I’m just too worn down to even get to the point where I’d know.\n\nI guess I’m not looking forward to stuff much, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not enjoying it. Sometimes I feel like if someone put me in the situation, maybe I’d enjoy parts of it, but I’m not really seeking it out. So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes and make it sound clearer than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… the moving slowly part, definitely. I didn’t really notice it at first as a “symptom,” I just thought I was tired or distracted, but people at work started saying things like I looked slowed down, or asking if I was okay because I wasn’t responding like I normally do.\n\nAnd I could feel it too, once I paid attention. Even really basic things were taking me way longer than they should. Like putting a blood pressure cuff away after using it, something I’ve done a million times, I’d just be standing there doing it so slowly, like my hands weren’t catching up with what I was trying to do. Or if someone asked me a simple question, I’d need this weird extra amount of time to process it and answer, even if I knew the answer.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because in that kind of work you’re supposed to be quick and alert, and I felt like everyone could see I was lagging behind. I don’t think it was so much that I couldn’t sit still. It was more like everything in me got slowed down.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you been feeling this way with the fatigue and slowed-down feeling—has it been going on for two weeks or more?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Yeah… it’s been long enough that it doesn’t feel like just one bad day or like I didn’t sleep well once. I’m here because my body feels completely drained, like even walking from my car to the clinic takes way more out of me than it should.

And at work, even opening the EHR feels like this whole task sometimes. So… yeah, it’s not just moment-to-moment tired. It’s been going on for a while, and it feels constant enough that I finally felt like I couldn’t just keep pushing through it.

Even really basic things were taking me way longer than they should. Like putting a blood pressure cuff away after using it, something I’ve done a million times, I’d just be standing there doing it so slowly, like my hands weren’t catching up with what I was trying to do. Or if someone asked me a simple question, I’d need this weird extra amount of time to process it and answer, even if I knew the answer.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because in that kind of work you’re supposed to be quick and alert, and I felt like everyone could see I was lagging behind. I don’t think it was so much that I couldn’t sit still. It was more like everything in me got slowed down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way with the fatigue and slowed-down feeling—has it been going on for two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been long enough that it doesn’t feel like just one bad day or like I didn’t sleep well once. Like I’ll sit there staring at it, knowing exactly what I need to do, but it feels like my body and brain are dragging through mud just to get started. It’s embarrassing because those are basic things, and I keep thinking, why is this taking so much effort?\n\nSo… yeah, it’s not just moment-to-moment tired. It’s been going on for a while, and it feels constant enough that I finally felt like I couldn’t just keep pushing through it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling like you’re a failure or that you’re not good enough, or have you been hard on yourself in a way that feels different from before?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yeah, I’ve been really hard on myself. More than usual, I think. It’s not just like, “Oh, I made a mistake,” or normal stress about work. It’s like I keep going back over everything I did, over and over, and I can’t let it go.

Especially with patients. I’ll replay every chart I touched, every set of vitals I took, every little thing I documented, and I’ll start thinking, what if I missed something, what if I put something in wrong, what if I hurt someone and nobody realized it yet. Even when my supervisors tell me nothing bad happened, or that I did what I was supposed to do, I still don’t really believe it. I’ll hear them say it, and for a second I want to accept it, but then my mind just goes right back to, “No, you probably messed up somehow.”

It feels like I’m not safe to be trusted with things, I guess. And that’s hard to say, because I know I care about doing a good job. I really do. But lately it feels like caring isn’t enough, like I’m still failing people somehow. I feel guilty even when there isn’t a clear reason for it.

started feeling empty and hopeless most days, like I had already ruined my future in healthcare before it even really started.\n- key: S026\n  name: Weight_Appetite_Change\n  description: Significant or water.\n- key: S027\n  name: Sleep_Disturbance\n  description: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.\n  manifestation: For a while I could sleep only two or three hours and still feel wired, but lately I either wake up before dawn feeling sick with worry or sleep half the day and still feel exhausted.\n- key: S028\n  name: Psychomotor_Changes\n  description: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.\n  manifestation: People at work said I looked slowed down, and I could feel it too because even putting a blood pressure cuff away or answering a question took me way longer than normal.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep replaying every chart and every set of vitals in my head, feeling like I hurt patients even when my supervisors tell me nothing bad happened.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: Some days I felt amazing and unstoppable, then if someone corrected my documentation or told me to slow down I would snap at them like they were insulting me.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: I had so much energy that I couldn't sit still, so I kept walking the halls, reorganizing supplies, checking rooms, and looking for more things to do even after my shift should have been over.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: I really believed I understood patient care better than the nurses and managers and that if they listened to me I could change the whole system.\n- key: S037\n  name: Pressured_Speech\n  description: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: Once I started explaining my ideas about the clinic, I talked so fast and so long that people would try to interrupt and I still couldn't make myself stop.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: When I was trying to chart, every hallway noise, phone ring, or movement outside the door pulled me away, and I would forget what patient I was even documenting.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: I started making new workflow plans, messaging coworkers after hours, offering to train people I was barely ahead of, and taking on extra tasks until I couldn't finish the basic ones safely.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the manic episode\n  manifestation: The ideas about having a special medical mission and hearing my name or monitors calling me only happened when I was wired, barely sleeping, and trying to overhaul the clinic. I also had to stop preparing seriously for my CNA certification because I couldn't keep up.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: The whole crisis built up over about two months after my clinic changed my responsibilities.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 9 months\n  manifestation: The depressed, guilty, exhausted part has lasted about nine months since I crashed after that high-energy period.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 18 / healthcare_support_worker / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S029\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… the moving slowly part, definitely. I didn’t really notice it at first as a “symptom,” I just thought I was tired or distracted, but people at work started saying things like I looked slowed down, or asking if I was okay because I wasn’t responding like I normally do.\n\nAnd I could feel it too, once I paid attention. Even really basic things were taking me way longer than they should. Like putting a blood pressure cuff away after using it, something I’ve done a million times, I’d just be standing there doing it so slowly, like my hands weren’t catching up with what I was trying to do. Or if someone asked me a simple question, I’d need this weird extra amount of time to process it and answer, even if I knew the answer.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because in that kind of work you’re supposed to be quick and alert, and I felt like everyone could see I was lagging behind. I don’t think it was so much that I couldn’t sit still. I’m here because my body feels completely drained, like even walking from my car to the clinic takes way more out of me than it should.\n\nAnd at work, even opening the EHR feels like this whole task sometimes. Like I’ll sit there staring at it, knowing exactly what I need to do, but it feels like my body and brain are dragging through mud just to get started. It’s embarrassing because those are basic things, and I keep thinking, why is this taking so much effort?\n\nSo… yeah, it’s not just moment-to-moment tired. It’s been going on for a while, and it feels constant enough that I finally felt like I couldn’t just keep pushing through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re a failure or that you’re not good enough, or have you been hard on yourself in a way that feels different from before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I’ve been really hard on myself. More than usual, I think. It’s not just like, “Oh, I made a mistake,” or normal stress about work. It’s like I keep going back over everything I did, over and over, and I can’t let it go.\n\nEspecially with patients. I’ll replay every chart I touched, every set of vitals I took, every little thing I documented, and I’ll start thinking, what if I missed something, what if I put something in wrong, what if I hurt someone and nobody realized it yet. Even when my supervisors tell me nothing bad happened, or that I did what I was supposed to do, I still don’t really believe it. I’ll hear them say it, and for a second I want to accept it, but then my mind just goes right back to, “No, you probably messed up somehow.”\n\nIt feels like I’m not safe to be trusted with things, I guess. And that’s hard to say, because I know I care about doing a good job. I really do. But lately it feels like caring isn’t enough, like I’m still failing people somehow. I feel guilty even when there isn’t a clear reason for it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced times when things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with friends, felt completely meaningless or brought you no pleasure at all?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I don’t know if I’d say completely meaningless. I mean… I haven’t really been doing much, so it’s hard to compare. It’s not like I sat down with a hobby and thought, “This brings me no pleasure.” It’s more that I don’t even really get to the point of doing things the way I normally would.

Like if someone asks me to hang out, I kind of just… don’t have the energy to think through it. But I’m not sure if that’s because I don’t enjoy people anymore or because everything feels like a lot right now. I guess when I’m actually around people, I’m not sitting there thinking it’s meaningless, but I’m also not exactly feeling normal about it either.

So… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear thing where all my hobbies or friends feel pointless. It’s more just that I haven’t felt up for much.

It’s like my whole body is heavy and I have to force myself through every basic thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like hobbies, socializing, or activities you found pleasurable before?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I haven’t really sat down and thought, like, “Do I still enjoy this or not?” It’s more that everything feels kind of hard to get myself to do, so I don’t know if that means I’ve lost interest or if I’m just… I don’t know, not in a place where I’m paying attention to what I enjoy.\n\nLike, if you asked me what sounds fun right now, I’d probably have trouble coming up with something, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not caring about things anymore. I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down, sad, or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. It’s not like I’m crying all day or anything, but most days I wake up and there’s this really heavy empty feeling, like there’s nothing in me. And once my energy dropped the way it did, it started feeling hopeless, like I’d already messed everything up.\n\nI keep thinking I ruined my future in healthcare before it even really started. Like, I’m only 18, I know that, but it feels like I should be able to handle things better, and instead I’m barely getting through basic stuff. So then I start thinking, if I can’t even function now, how am I supposed to build a real career or become someone people can rely on?\n\nIt’s there most of the day, almost every day lately. Even when I’m around people or doing what I’m supposed to do, underneath it I just feel kind of empty and like there isn’t much point because I already fell behind or damaged my chances somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or time with friends, don’t feel as rewarding or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m honestly not sure I can say it that strongly, like “completely uninteresting” or “no enjoyment at all.” I know that’s probably frustrating because it’s kind of a yes-or-no question, but it doesn’t feel that clear to me.

It’s more like… I haven’t really been reaching for things. Like if there’s something I normally might do, I don’t get that same pull toward it, but I also don’t know if that means it doesn’t feel good anymore or if I’m just too drained to even start. Sometimes the thought of making plans or doing something I’d usually like feels like another task, if that makes sense.

With friends, I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “I don’t care about these people,” or that it’s totally pointless. It’s more like I don’t have much in me to initiate or keep up. If someone asked me, I might still go along with it, but I don’t know that I’d be excited ahead of time the way I used to be.

So… I guess my answer is I haven’t really noticed a clean “nothing is enjoyable anymore” thing. It’s more that I’m not doing much and I don’t feel up for much, and I’m not totally sure what that means.

I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear thing where all my hobbies or friends feel pointless. It’s more just that I haven’t felt up for much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to look forward to, like hobbies or spending time with friends, feel completely uninteresting or bring you no enjoyment at all?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure I can say it that strongly, like “completely uninteresting” or “no enjoyment at all.” I know that’s probably frustrating because it’s kind of a yes-or-no question, but it doesn’t feel that clear to me.\n\nIt’s more like… I haven’t really been reaching for things. Like if there’s something I normally might do, I don’t get that same pull toward it, but I also don’t know if that means it doesn’t feel good anymore or if I’m just too drained to even start. Sometimes the thought of making plans or doing something I’d usually like feels like another task, if that makes sense.\n\nWith friends, I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “I don’t care about these people,” or that it’s totally pointless. It’s more like I don’t have much in me to initiate or keep up. If someone asked me, I might still go along with it, but I don’t know that I’d be excited ahead of time the way I used to be.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I haven’t really noticed a clean “nothing is enjoyable anymore” thing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m still kind of unsure, honestly. I know you’re asking about when I actually do the thing, not just whether I feel like starting it, and that’s the part I’m having trouble sorting out.

Like, I haven’t really been doing enough normal stuff to have a clear comparison. If I end up around friends, I don’t think I feel nothing. It’s not like I’m sitting there completely blank or thinking, “This is pointless, I hate this.” Sometimes it’s okay in the moment, or at least distracting for a little while. But I also don’t feel like I’m fully there the way I used to be, if that makes sense.

With hobbies, it’s similar. I’m not sure I can say they don’t feel pleasurable anymore, because I haven’t been consistently doing them and then noticing, “Oh, this gives me no enjoyment.” It’s more like everything takes effort before I even get to that point, so I don’t really know if the enjoyment is gone or if I’m just not getting myself into situations where I’d notice it.

So I guess… no, I haven’t clearly noticed that things are completely not pleasurable or interesting once I’m doing them. It’s more uncertain than that.

Like I’ll sit there staring at it, knowing exactly what I need to do, but it feels like my body and brain are dragging through mud just to get started. It’s embarrassing because those are basic things, and I keep thinking, why is this taking so much effort?\n\nSo… yeah, it’s not just moment-to-moment tired. It’s been going on for a while, and it feels constant enough that I finally felt like I couldn’t just keep pushing through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re a failure or that you’re not good enough, or have you been hard on yourself in a way that feels different from before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I’ve been really hard on myself. More than usual, I think. It’s not just like, “Oh, I made a mistake,” or normal stress about work. It’s like I keep going back over everything I did, over and over, and I can’t let it go.\n\nEspecially with patients. I’ll replay every chart I touched, every set of vitals I took, every little thing I documented, and I’ll start thinking, what if I missed something, what if I put something in wrong, what if I hurt someone and nobody realized it yet. Even when my supervisors tell me nothing bad happened, or that I did what I was supposed to do, I still don’t really believe it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m trying to think of a specific time, and I don’t know that I can really point to one clearly.

Like, in the past couple weeks, there have been little moments where I’ve been around people or kind of half-done something I normally might like, but I don’t remember having this clear feeling of, “Wow, this should be enjoyable and it isn’t.” It’s more muddled than that. A lot of the time I don’t get very far into doing anything in the first place, so it’s hard for me to judge the “even then” part.

If I was with someone, I might have been kind of quiet or not as into it, but I don’t know if I’d say it didn’t feel good at all. It may have been fine for a bit, or just not enough for me to really notice. And with hobbies, I can’t think of a solid example where I sat down and did it like normal and felt nothing from it.

So… looking back, no, I can’t really recall a definite time like that. Not in a way I’d feel confident saying yes to.

And once my energy dropped the way it did, it started feeling hopeless, like I’d already messed everything up.\n\nI keep thinking I ruined my future in healthcare before it even really started. Like, I’m only 18, I know that, but it feels like I should be able to handle things better, and instead I’m barely getting through basic stuff. So then I start thinking, if I can’t even function now, how am I supposed to build a real career or become someone people can rely on?\n\nIt’s there most of the day, almost every day lately. Even when I’m around people or doing what I’m supposed to do, underneath it I just feel kind of empty and like there isn’t much point because I already fell behind or damaged my chances somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or time with friends, don’t feel as rewarding or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. Like, I’m trying to think of examples, but I feel kind of blank when you ask it that way.\n\nI haven’t really been paying attention to whether things feel “rewarding” or not. It’s more like I’ll think about doing something with friends or something I normally would do, and then I’m already tired just thinking about getting myself there, or making conversation, or acting normal. So I can’t tell if the thing itself doesn’t feel good anymore, or if I’m just too worn down to even get to the point where I’d know.\n\nI guess I’m not looking forward to stuff much, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not enjoying it. Sometimes I feel like if someone put me in the situation, maybe I’d enjoy parts of it, but I’m not really seeking it out. So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes and make it sound clearer than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… the moving slowly part, definitely. I didn’t really notice it at first as a “symptom,” I just thought I was tired or distracted, but people at work started saying things like I looked slowed down, or asking if I was okay because I wasn’t responding like I normally do.\n\nAnd I could feel it too, once I paid attention. Even really basic things were taking me way longer than they should. Like putting a blood pressure cuff away after using it, something I’ve done a million times, I’d just be standing there doing it so slowly, like my hands weren’t catching up with what I was trying to do. Or if someone asked me a simple question, I’d need this weird extra amount of time to process it and answer, even if I knew the answer.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because in that kind of work you’re supposed to be quick and alert, and I felt like everyone could see I was lagging behind. I don’t think it was so much that I couldn’t sit still. It was more like everything in me got slowed down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way with the fatigue and slowed-down feeling—has it been going on for two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been long enough that it doesn’t feel like just one bad day or like I didn’t sleep well once. I’m here because my body feels completely drained, like even walking from my car to the clinic takes way more out of me than it should.\n\nAnd at work, even opening the EHR feels like this whole task sometimes. Like I’ll sit there staring at it, knowing exactly what I need to do, but it feels like my body and brain are dragging through mud just to get started. It’s embarrassing because those are basic things, and I keep thinking, why is this taking so much effort?\n\nSo… yeah, it’s not just moment-to-moment tired. It’s been going on for a while, and it feels constant enough that I finally felt like I couldn’t just keep pushing through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re a failure or that you’re not good enough, or have you been hard on yourself in a way that feels different from before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I’ve been really hard on myself. More than usual, I think. It’s not just like, “Oh, I made a mistake,” or normal stress about work. It’s like I keep going back over everything I did, over and over, and I can’t let it go.\n\nEspecially with patients. I’ll replay every chart I touched, every set of vitals I took, every little thing I documented, and I’ll start thinking, what if I missed something, what if I put something in wrong, what if I hurt someone and nobody realized it yet. Even when my supervisors tell me nothing bad happened, or that I did what I was supposed to do, I still don’t really believe it. I’ll hear them say it, and for a second I want to accept it, but then my mind just goes right back to, “No, you probably messed up somehow.”\n\nIt feels like I’m not safe to be trusted with things, I guess. And that’s hard to say, because I know I care about doing a good job. I really do. But lately it feels like caring isn’t enough, like I’m still failing people somehow. I feel guilty even when there isn’t a clear reason for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced times when things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with friends, felt completely meaningless or brought you no pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely meaningless. Yeah… I mean, yes, there have been times like that, at least the “high” and irritable part. I don’t know how to explain it without sounding dramatic, but some days I’d feel amazing, like I was just on top of everything and could handle anything. Like unstoppable, almost. I’d be moving fast, talking fast, feeling like I was doing better than everyone expected of me.

And then the weird thing is, if someone tried to slow me down or correct me, especially at work, I would take it really personally. Like if someone corrected my documentation or told me, “Hey, slow down,” I wouldn’t just be like, “Okay, thanks.” I’d snap at them, like they were insulting me or trying to make me look stupid. Even if they were probably just doing their job.

It felt like one second I was feeling incredible and confident, and the next second I was irritated, like everyone was in my way or talking down to me. I don’t love admitting that, because I know it probably made me hard to be around, but yeah, that has happened.

It’s been going on for a while, and it feels constant enough that I finally felt like I couldn’t just keep pushing through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re a failure or that you’re not good enough, or have you been hard on yourself in a way that feels different from before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I’ve been really hard on myself. More than usual, I think. It’s not just like, “Oh, I made a mistake,” or normal stress about work. It’s like I keep going back over everything I did, over and over, and I can’t let it go.\n\nEspecially with patients. I’ll replay every chart I touched, every set of vitals I took, every little thing I documented, and I’ll start thinking, what if I missed something, what if I put something in wrong, what if I hurt someone and nobody realized it yet. Even when my supervisors tell me nothing bad happened, or that I did what I was supposed to do, I still don’t really believe it. I’ll hear them say it, and for a second I want to accept it, but then my mind just goes right back to, “No, you probably messed up somehow.”\n\nIt feels like I’m not safe to be trusted with things, I guess. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s hard for me to count it like “how many times,” because it kind of blended together instead of being one clean episode with a start and stop. The way I remember it, the whole thing really built up over maybe about two months, after my clinic changed what I was responsible for.

At first it didn’t feel like a crisis or anything. It felt more like I was just adjusting, or like I had to prove I could handle the new stuff. But then over those weeks, it kept ramping up. Some days were more intense than others, and I’d have those stretches where I felt really keyed up or unusually confident, or I’d get irritated way faster than normal. But it wasn’t like, “Oh, this lasted exactly three days and then ended.” It was more like the pressure and the way I was acting kept building and building across that couple-month period.

So I guess the best answer is: not something I can neatly count, but the main period I’m thinking of lasted around two months from when my responsibilities at the clinic changed.

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Looking back, can you recall a time in the past two weeks when you actually did something you used to enjoy, like a hobby or time with friends, and even then it didn’t feel good or interesting to you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a specific time, and I don’t know that I can really point to one clearly.\n\nLike, in the past couple weeks, there have been little moments where I’ve been around people or kind of half-done something I normally might like, but I don’t remember having this clear feeling of, “Wow, this should be enjoyable and it isn’t.” It’s more muddled than that. A lot of the time I don’t get very far into doing anything in the first place, so it’s hard for me to judge the “even then” part.\n\nIf I was with someone, I might have been kind of quiet or not as into it, but I don’t know if I’d say it didn’t feel good at all. It may have been fine for a bit, or just not enough for me to really notice. And with hobbies, I can’t think of a solid example where I sat down and did it like normal and felt nothing from it.\n\nSo… looking back, no, I can’t really recall a definite time like that. Not in a way I’d feel confident saying yes to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt unusually high, overly energetic, or extremely irritable, where you were doing a lot more than usual, maybe not sleeping much but still feeling rested, or getting involved in risky behaviors or projects way beyond what you’d normally take on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes, there have been times like that, at least the “high” and irritable part. I don’t know how to explain it without sounding dramatic, but some days I’d feel amazing, like I was just on top of everything and could handle anything. Like unstoppable, almost. I’d be moving fast, talking fast, feeling like I was doing better than everyone expected of me.\n\nAnd then the weird thing is, if someone tried to slow me down or correct me, especially at work, I would take it really personally. Like if someone corrected my documentation or told me, “Hey, slow down,” I wouldn’t just be like, “Okay, thanks.” I’d snap at them, like they were insulting me or trying to make me look stupid. Even if they were probably just doing their job.\n\nIt felt like one second I was feeling incredible and confident, and the next second I was irritated, like everyone was in my way or talking down to me. I don’t love admitting that, because I know it probably made me hard to be around, but yeah, that has happened.\n\nDoctor: How often have you experienced those high or irritable periods, and how long do they usually last when they happen?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to count it like “how many times,” because it kind of blended together instead of being one clean episode with a start and stop. The way I remember it, the whole thing really built up over maybe about two months, after my clinic changed what I was responsible for.\n\nAt first it didn’t feel like a crisis or anything. It felt more like I was just adjusting, or like I had to prove I could handle the new stuff. But then over those weeks, it kept ramping up. Some days were more intense than others, and I’d have those stretches where I felt really keyed up or unusually confident, or I’d get irritated way faster than normal. But it wasn’t like, “Oh, this lasted exactly three days and then ended.” It was more like the pressure and the way I was acting kept building and building across that couple-month period.\n\nSo I guess the best answer is: not something I can neatly count, but the main period I’m thinking of lasted around two months from when my responsibilities at the clinic changed.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you tell me how long the most intense part of that high or irritable period lasted—like, from when you really started feeling that way to when it finally settled down?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 18 / healthcare_support_worker / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"The whole crisis built up over about two months after my clinic changed my responsibilities.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
The most intense part… I still think of it as part of that whole stretch, not like a separate little window I can cleanly mark off.

It really started after my clinic changed my responsibilities. At first I kept telling myself I was just trying to keep up or prove I could handle it, so I didn’t label it as anything unusual right away. But looking back, that’s when it began building. Then over the next weeks it kept getting stronger, like I was more keyed up, more reactive, more caught up in it.

So from when it really started feeling that way to when it finally settled down, I’d say it was about two months altogether. The “crisis” part didn’t just happen overnight—it kind of escalated across that whole two-month period after the change at work.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I just feel completely drained, like my body is running on nothing. Even little things feel huge right now—walking from my car into the clinic felt like it took everything I had, and at work, even just opening the EHR and trying to get started feels exhausting. It’s not just “I’m tired” in the normal way. It’s like my whole body is heavy and I have to force myself through every basic thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like hobbies, socializing, or activities you found pleasurable before?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I haven’t really sat down and thought, like, “Do I still enjoy this or not?” It’s more that everything feels kind of hard to get myself to do, so I don’t know if that means I’ve lost interest or if I’m just… I don’t know, not in a place where I’m paying attention to what I enjoy.\n\nLike, if you asked me what sounds fun right now, I’d probably have trouble coming up with something, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not caring about things anymore. I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down, sad, or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. Even when I’m around people or doing what I’m supposed to do, underneath it I just feel kind of empty and like there isn’t much point because I already fell behind or damaged my chances somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or time with friends, don’t feel as rewarding or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. Like, I’m trying to think of examples, but I feel kind of blank when you ask it that way.\n\nI haven’t really been paying attention to whether things feel “rewarding” or not. It’s more like I’ll think about doing something with friends or something I normally would do, and then I’m already tired just thinking about getting myself there, or making conversation, or acting normal. So I can’t tell if the thing itself doesn’t feel good anymore, or if I’m just too worn down to even get to the point where I’d know.\n\nI guess I’m not looking forward to stuff much, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not enjoying it. Sometimes I feel like if someone put me in the situation, maybe I’d enjoy parts of it, but I’m not really seeking it out. So… I’m not sure. Even really basic things were taking me way longer than they should. Like putting a blood pressure cuff away after using it, something I’ve done a million times, I’d just be standing there doing it so slowly, like my hands weren’t catching up with what I was trying to do. Or if someone asked me a simple question, I’d need this weird extra amount of time to process it and answer, even if I knew the answer.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because in that kind of work you’re supposed to be quick and alert, and I felt like everyone could see I was lagging behind. I don’t think it was so much that I couldn’t sit still. It was more like everything in me got slowed down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way with the fatigue and slowed-down feeling—has it been going on for two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been long enough that it doesn’t feel like just one bad day or like I didn’t sleep well once. I’m here because my body feels completely drained, like even walking from my car to the clinic takes way more out of me than it should.\n\nAnd at work, even opening the EHR feels like this whole task sometimes. Like I’ll sit there staring at it, knowing exactly what I need to do, but it feels like my body and brain are dragging through mud just to get started. It’s embarrassing because those are basic things, and I keep thinking, why is this taking so much effort?\n\nSo… yeah, it’s not just moment-to-moment tired. It’s been going on for a while, and it feels constant enough that I finally felt like I couldn’t just keep pushing through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re a failure or that you’re not good enough, or have you been hard on yourself in a way that feels different from before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I’ve been really hard on myself. More than usual, I think. It’s not just like, “Oh, I made a mistake,” or normal stress about work. It’s like I keep going back over everything I did, over and over, and I can’t let it go.\n\nEspecially with patients. I’ll replay every chart I touched, every set of vitals I took, every little thing I documented, and I’ll start thinking, what if I missed something, what if I put something in wrong, what if I hurt someone and nobody realized it yet. Even when my supervisors tell me nothing bad happened, or that I did what I was supposed to do, I still don’t really believe it. I’ll hear them say it, and for a second I want to accept it, but then my mind just goes right back to, “No, you probably messed up somehow.”\n\nIt feels like I’m not safe to be trusted with things, I guess. And that’s hard to say, because I know I care about doing a good job. I really do. But lately it feels like caring isn’t enough, like I’m still failing people somehow. I feel guilty even when there isn’t a clear reason for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced times when things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with friends, felt completely meaningless or brought you no pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely meaningless. I mean… I haven’t really been doing much, so it’s hard to compare. It’s not like I sat down with a hobby and thought, “This brings me no pleasure.” It’s more that I don’t even really get to the point of doing things the way I normally would.\n\nLike if someone asks me to hang out, I kind of just… don’t have the energy to think through it. But I’m not sure if that’s because I don’t enjoy people anymore or because everything feels like a lot right now. I guess when I’m actually around people, I’m not sitting there thinking it’s meaningless, but I’m also not exactly feeling normal about it either.\n\nSo… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear thing where all my hobbies or friends feel pointless. It’s more just that I haven’t felt up for much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to look forward to, like hobbies or spending time with friends, feel completely uninteresting or bring you no enjoyment at all?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure I can say it that strongly, like “completely uninteresting” or “no enjoyment at all.” I know that’s probably frustrating because it’s kind of a yes-or-no question, but it doesn’t feel that clear to me.\n\nIt’s more like… I haven’t really been reaching for things. Like if there’s something I normally might do, I don’t get that same pull toward it, but I also don’t know if that means it doesn’t feel good anymore or if I’m just too drained to even start. Sometimes the thought of making plans or doing something I’d usually like feels like another task, if that makes sense.\n\nWith friends, I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “I don’t care about these people,” or that it’s totally pointless. It’s more like I don’t have much in me to initiate or keep up. If someone asked me, I might still go along with it, but I don’t know that I’d be excited ahead of time the way I used to be.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I haven’t really noticed a clean “nothing is enjoyable anymore” thing. It’s more that I’m not doing much and I don’t feel up for much, and I’m not totally sure what that means.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with friends, don't feel pleasurable or interesting to you anymore, even when you manage to do them?\n\nPatient: I’m still kind of unsure, honestly. I know you’re asking about when I actually do the thing, not just whether I feel like starting it, and that’s the part I’m having trouble sorting out.\n\nLike, I haven’t really been doing enough normal stuff to have a clear comparison. If I end up around friends, I don’t think I feel nothing. It’s not like I’m sitting there completely blank or thinking, “This is pointless, I hate this.” Sometimes it’s okay in the moment, or at least distracting for a little while. Even if they were probably just doing their job.\n\nIt felt like one second I was feeling incredible and confident, and the next second I was irritated, like everyone was in my way or talking down to me. I feel guilty even when there isn’t a clear reason for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced times when things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with friends, felt completely meaningless or brought you no pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely meaningless. I mean… I haven’t really been doing much, so it’s hard to compare. It’s not like I sat down with a hobby and thought, “This brings me no pleasure.” It’s more that I don’t even really get to the point of doing things the way I normally would.\n\nLike if someone asks me to hang out, I kind of just… don’t have the energy to think through it. But I’m not sure if that’s because I don’t enjoy people anymore or because everything feels like a lot right now. I guess when I’m actually around people, I’m not sitting there thinking it’s meaningless, but I’m also not exactly feeling normal about it either.\n\nSo… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear thing where all my hobbies or friends feel pointless. It’s more just that I haven’t felt up for much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to look forward to, like hobbies or spending time with friends, feel completely uninteresting or bring you no enjoyment at all?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure I can say it that strongly, like “completely uninteresting” or “no enjoyment at all.” I know that’s probably frustrating because it’s kind of a yes-or-no question, but it doesn’t feel that clear to me.\n\nIt’s more like… I haven’t really been reaching for things. Like if there’s something I normally might do, I don’t get that same pull toward it, but I also don’t know if that means it doesn’t feel good anymore or if I’m just too drained to even start. Sometimes the thought of making plans or doing something I’d usually like feels like another task, if that makes sense.\n\nWith friends, I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “I don’t care about these people,” or that it’s totally pointless. It’s more like I don’t have much in me to initiate or keep up. If someone asked me, I might still go along with it, but I don’t know that I’d be excited ahead of time the way I used to be.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I haven’t really noticed a clean “nothing is enjoyable anymore” thing. It’s more that I’m not doing much and I don’t feel up for much, and I’m not totally sure what that means.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with friends, don't feel pleasurable or interesting to you anymore, even when you manage to do them?\n\nPatient: I’m still kind of unsure, honestly. I know you’re asking about when I actually do the thing, not just whether I feel like starting it, and that’s the part I’m having trouble sorting out.\n\nLike, I haven’t really been doing enough normal stuff to have a clear comparison. If I end up around friends, I don’t think I feel nothing. It’s not like I’m sitting there completely blank or thinking, “This is pointless, I hate this.” Sometimes it’s okay in the moment, or at least distracting for a little while. But I also don’t feel like I’m fully there the way I used to be, if that makes sense.\n\nWith hobbies, it’s similar. Yeah… I think so. Not just like a random bad mood for a few hours. There were stretches where, for several days in a row, I felt really different from my normal self.

Some days I’d wake up and feel amazing, like I was completely on top of things, like nothing could really touch me. I remember feeling almost unstoppable, like I was moving through the day with this huge amount of confidence. And at the time I didn’t think, “This is unusual.” I thought I was just doing really well, or finally handling things the way I was supposed to.

But then it would flip really fast into irritation. Like if someone at work corrected my documentation, or even just told me to slow down, I’d feel this rush of anger, like they were insulting me or trying to make me look incompetent. I’d snap back in a way that was more intense than the situation deserved. Looking back, that wasn’t my usual self. I mean, I can get annoyed like anyone, but this was different—it felt like every correction was personal, like people were in my way.

So yes, there were definitely several-day periods where I was unusually high and confident, and then really irritable if anyone challenged me or tried to slow me down.

Like, I’m only 18, I know that, but it feels like I should be able to handle things better, and instead I’m barely getting through basic stuff. So then I start thinking, if I can’t even function now, how am I supposed to build a real career or become someone people can rely on?\n\nIt’s there most of the day, almost every day lately. Even when I’m around people or doing what I’m supposed to do, underneath it I just feel kind of empty and like there isn’t much point because I already fell behind or damaged my chances somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or time with friends, don’t feel as rewarding or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that for sure. Like, I’m trying to think of examples, but I feel kind of blank when you ask it that way.\n\nI haven’t really been paying attention to whether things feel “rewarding” or not. It’s more like I’ll think about doing something with friends or something I normally would do, and then I’m already tired just thinking about getting myself there, or making conversation, or acting normal. So I can’t tell if the thing itself doesn’t feel good anymore, or if I’m just too worn down to even get to the point where I’d know.\n\nI guess I’m not looking forward to stuff much, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not enjoying it. Sometimes I feel like if someone put me in the situation, maybe I’d enjoy parts of it, but I’m not really seeking it out. So… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to say yes and make it sound clearer than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… the moving slowly part, definitely. I didn’t really notice it at first as a “symptom,” I just thought I was tired or distracted, but people at work started saying things like I looked slowed down, or asking if I was okay because I wasn’t responding like I normally do.\n\nAnd I could feel it too, once I paid attention. Even really basic things were taking me way longer than they should. Like putting a blood pressure cuff away after using it, something I’ve done a million times, I’d just be standing there doing it so slowly, like my hands weren’t catching up with what I was trying to do. Or if someone asked me a simple question, I’d need this weird extra amount of time to process it and answer, even if I knew the answer.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because in that kind of work you’re supposed to be quick and alert, and I felt like everyone could see I was lagging behind. I don’t think it was so much that I couldn’t sit still. It was more like everything in me got slowed down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way with the fatigue and slowed-down feeling—has it been going on for two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been long enough that it doesn’t feel like just one bad day or like I didn’t sleep well once. I’m here because my body feels completely drained, like even walking from my car to the clinic takes way more out of me than it should.\n\nAnd at work, even opening the EHR feels like this whole task sometimes. Like I’ll sit there staring at it, knowing exactly what I need to do, but it feels like my body and brain are dragging through mud just to get started. It’s embarrassing because those are basic things, and I keep thinking, why is this taking so much effort?\n\nSo… yeah, it’s not just moment-to-moment tired. It’s been going on for a while, and it feels constant enough that I finally felt like I couldn’t just keep pushing through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re a failure or that you’re not good enough, or have you been hard on yourself in a way that feels different from before?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I’ve been really hard on myself. More than usual, I think. It’s not just like, “Oh, I made a mistake,” or normal stress about work. It’s like I keep going back over everything I did, over and over, and I can’t let it go.\n\nEspecially with patients. I’ll replay every chart I touched, every set of vitals I took, every little thing I documented, and I’ll start thinking, what if I missed something, what if I put something in wrong, what if I hurt someone and nobody realized it yet. Even when my supervisors tell me nothing bad happened, or that I did what I was supposed to do, I still don’t really believe it. I’ll hear them say it, and for a second I want to accept it, but then my mind just goes right back to, “No, you probably messed up somehow.”\n\nIt feels like I’m not safe to be trusted with things, I guess. And that’s hard to say, because I know I care about doing a good job. I really do. But lately it feels like caring isn’t enough, like I’m still failing people somehow. I feel guilty even when there isn’t a clear reason for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced times when things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with friends, felt completely meaningless or brought you no pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely meaningless. I mean… I haven’t really been doing much, so it’s hard to compare. It’s not like I sat down with a hobby and thought, “This brings me no pleasure.” It’s more that I don’t even really get to the point of doing things the way I normally would.\n\nLike if someone asks me to hang out, I kind of just… don’t have the energy to think through it. But I’m not sure if that’s because I don’t enjoy people anymore or because everything feels like a lot right now. I guess when I’m actually around people, I’m not sitting there thinking it’s meaningless, but I’m also not exactly feeling normal about it either.\n\nSo… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear thing where all my hobbies or friends feel pointless. It’s more just that I haven’t felt up for much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to look forward to, like hobbies or spending time with friends, feel completely uninteresting or bring you no enjoyment at all?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure I can say it that strongly, like “completely uninteresting” or “no enjoyment at all.” I know that’s probably frustrating because it’s kind of a yes-or-no question, but it doesn’t feel that clear to me.\n\nIt’s more like… I haven’t really been reaching for things. Like if there’s something I normally might do, I don’t get that same pull toward it, but I also don’t know if that means it doesn’t feel good anymore or if I’m just too drained to even start. Sometimes the thought of making plans or doing something I’d usually like feels like another task, if that makes sense.\n\nWith friends, I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “I don’t care about these people,” or that it’s totally pointless. It’s more like I don’t have much in me to initiate or keep up. If someone asked me, I might still go along with it, but I don’t know that I’d be excited ahead of time the way I used to be.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I haven’t really noticed a clean “nothing is enjoyable anymore” thing. It’s more that I’m not doing much and I don’t feel up for much, and I’m not totally sure what that means.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with friends, don't feel pleasurable or interesting to you anymore, even when you manage to do them?\n\nPatient: I’m still kind of unsure, honestly. I know you’re asking about when I actually do the thing, not just whether I feel like starting it, and that’s the part I’m having trouble sorting out.\n\nLike, I haven’t really been doing enough normal stuff to have a clear comparison. If I end up around friends, I don’t think I feel nothing. It’s not like I’m sitting there completely blank or thinking, “This is pointless, I hate this.” Sometimes it’s okay in the moment, or at least distracting for a little while. But I also don’t feel like I’m fully there the way I used to be, if that makes sense.\n\nWith hobbies, it’s similar. I’m not sure I can say they don’t feel pleasurable anymore, because I haven’t been consistently doing them and then noticing, “Oh, this gives me no enjoyment.” It’s more like everything takes effort before I even get to that point, so I don’t really know if the enjoyment is gone or if I’m just not getting myself into situations where I’d notice it.\n\nSo I guess… no, I haven’t clearly noticed that things are completely not pleasurable or interesting once I’m doing them. It’s more uncertain than that.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, can you recall a time in the past two weeks when you actually did something you used to enjoy, like a hobby or time with friends, and even then it didn’t feel good or interesting to you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a specific time, and I don’t know that I can really point to one clearly.\n\nLike, in the past couple weeks, there have been little moments where I’ve been around people or kind of half-done something I normally might like, but I don’t remember having this clear feeling of, “Wow, this should be enjoyable and it isn’t.” It’s more muddled than that. A lot of the time I don’t get very far into doing anything in the first place, so it’s hard for me to judge the “even then” part.\n\nIf I was with someone, I might have been kind of quiet or not as into it, but I don’t know if I’d say it didn’t feel good at all. It may have been fine for a bit, or just not enough for me to really notice. And with hobbies, I can’t think of a solid example where I sat down and did it like normal and felt nothing from it.\n\nSo… looking back, no, I can’t really recall a definite time like that. Not in a way I’d feel confident saying yes to.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt unusually high, overly energetic, or extremely irritable, where you were doing a lot more than usual, maybe not sleeping much but still feeling rested, or getting involved in risky behaviors or projects way beyond what you’d normally take on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes, there have been times like that, at least the “high” and irritable part. I don’t know how to explain it without sounding dramatic, but some days I’d feel amazing, like I was just on top of everything and could handle anything. Like unstoppable, almost. I’d be moving fast, talking fast, feeling like I was doing better than everyone expected of me.\n\nAnd then the weird thing is, if someone tried to slow me down or correct me, especially at work, I would take it really personally. Like if someone corrected my documentation or told me, “Hey, slow down,” I wouldn’t just be like, “Okay, thanks.” I’d snap at them, like they were insulting me or trying to make me look stupid. Even if they were probably just doing their job.\n\nIt felt like one second I was feeling incredible and confident, and the next second I was irritated, like everyone was in my way or talking down to me. I don’t love admitting that, because I know it probably made me hard to be around, but yeah, that has happened.\n\nDoctor: How often have you experienced those high or irritable periods, and how long do they usually last when they happen?\n\nPatient: It’s hard for me to count it like “how many times,” because it kind of blended together instead of being one clean episode with a start and stop. The way I remember it, the whole thing really built up over maybe about two months, after my clinic changed what I was responsible for.\n\nAt first it didn’t feel like a crisis or anything. It felt more like I was just adjusting, or like I had to prove I could handle the new stuff. But then over those weeks, it kept ramping up. Some days were more intense than others, and I’d have those stretches where I felt really keyed up or unusually confident, or I’d get irritated way faster than normal. But it wasn’t like, “Oh, this lasted exactly three days and then ended.” It was more like the pressure and the way I was acting kept building and building across that couple-month period.\n\nSo I guess the best answer is: not something I can neatly count, but the main period I’m thinking of lasted around two months from when my responsibilities at the clinic changed.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me how long the most intense part of that high or irritable period lasted—like, from when you really started feeling that way to when it finally settled down?\n\nPatient: The most intense part… I still think of it as part of that whole stretch, not like a separate little window I can cleanly mark off.\n\nIt really started after my clinic changed my responsibilities. At first I kept telling myself I was just trying to keep up or prove I could handle it, so I didn’t label it as anything unusual right away. But looking back, that’s when it began building. Then over the next weeks it kept getting stronger, like I was more keyed up, more reactive, more caught up in it.\n\nSo from when it really started feeling that way to when it finally settled down, I’d say it was about two months altogether. The “crisis” part didn’t just happen overnight—it kind of escalated across that whole two-month period after the change at work.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was a time when you felt unusually high, irritable, or energetic for at least several days in a row—like a clear change from your usual self, not just brief moments or a single day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’d snap back in a way that was more intense than the situation deserved. Looking back, that wasn’t my usual self. I mean, I can get annoyed like anyone, but this was different—it felt like every correction was personal, like people were in my way.\n\nSo yes, there were definitely several-day periods where I was unusually high and confident, and then really irritable if anyone challenged me or tried to slow me down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But I also don’t feel like I’m fully there the way I used to be, if that makes sense.\n\nWith hobbies, it’s similar. I’m not sure I can say they don’t feel pleasurable anymore, because I haven’t been consistently doing them and then noticing, “Oh, this gives me no enjoyment.” It’s more like everything takes effort before I even get to that point, so I don’t really know if the enjoyment is gone or if I’m just not getting myself into situations where I’d notice it.\n\nSo I guess… no, I haven’t clearly noticed that things are completely not pleasurable or interesting once I’m doing them. It’s more uncertain than that.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, can you recall a time in the past two weeks when you actually did something you used to enjoy, like a hobby or time with friends, and even then it didn’t feel good or interesting to you?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a specific time, and I don’t know that I can really point to one clearly.\n\nLike, in the past couple weeks, there have been little moments where I’ve been around people or kind of half-done something I normally might like, but I don’t remember having this clear feeling of, “Wow, this should be enjoyable and it isn’t.” It’s more muddled than that. A lot of the time I don’t get very far into doing anything in the first place, so it’s hard for me to judge the “even then” part.\n\nIf I was with someone, I might have been kind of quiet or not as into it, but I don’t know if I’d say it didn’t feel good at all. I don’t know how to explain it without sounding dramatic, but some days I’d feel amazing, like I was just on top of everything and could handle anything. Like unstoppable, almost. I’d be moving fast, talking fast, feeling like I was doing better than everyone expected of me.\n\nAnd then the weird thing is, if someone tried to slow me down or correct me, especially at work, I would take it really personally. Like if someone corrected my documentation or told me, “Hey, slow down,” I wouldn’t just be like, “Okay, thanks.” I’d snap at them, like they were insulting me or trying to make me look stupid. But it wasn’t like, “Oh, this lasted exactly three days and then ended.” It was more like the pressure and the way I was acting kept building and building across that couple-month period.\n\nSo I guess the best answer is: not something I can neatly count, but the main period I’m thinking of lasted around two months from when my responsibilities at the clinic changed.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me how long the most intense part of that high or irritable period lasted—like, from when you really started feeling that way to when it finally settled down?\n\nPatient: The most intense part… I still think of it as part of that whole stretch, not like a separate little window I can cleanly mark off.\n\nIt really started after my clinic changed my responsibilities. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I know I already kind of answered that, but yes—there were times where it was definitely at least a full week, not just a day or two.

It wasn’t like I was calmly “in a good mood.” Some days I honestly felt amazing, like I was completely on top of everything, like I could just keep going and no one could really stop me. I remember feeling this confidence that was way bigger than normal for me—like I was unstoppable, I guess. At the time it didn’t seem like a problem. It felt like, “Finally, I’m doing well, I’m moving fast, I know what I’m doing.”

But then if someone corrected me, especially at work—like about my documentation—or told me to slow down, it would hit me completely wrong. I’d snap at them like they were insulting me or trying to embarrass me, even if they were probably just doing their job. It felt personal in the moment, like they were getting in my way or treating me like I was incompetent.

So yes, it lasted more than several days. There were full-week stretches where I was either feeling unusually great and unstoppable, or I was really irritable and reactive when people pushed back on me.

Yeah… yes. I know I’ve kind of said this already, but it wasn’t just like one rough day or me being in a good mood for a weekend. There were definitely stretches where, for at least a full week, I felt really different from how I normally am.
